tyle-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227613547"},{"@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227640399"},{"@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227869952"},{"@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227906233"},{"@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227962657"},{"@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228048887"},{"@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228081370"},{"@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228108488"},{"@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228135779"},{"@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228166519"},{"@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228238810"},{"@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228270582"},{"@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228301130"},{"@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228324552"},{"@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228349845"}],"inLanguage":"en-US","potentialAction":[{"@type":"ReadAction","target":["https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/"]}]},{"@type":"ImageObject","inLanguage":"en-US","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#primaryimage","url":"https:\/\/media.ereleases.com\/uploads\/2025\/03\/AP-style-for-press-releases-300x300.png","contentUrl":"https:\/\/media.ereleases.com\/uploads\/2025\/03\/AP-style-for-press-releases-300x300.png"},{"@type":"BreadcrumbList","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#breadcrumb","itemListElement":[{"@type":"ListItem","position":1,"name":"Home","item":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/"},{"@type":"ListItem","position":2,"name":"Industry release distribution service for small business, offering access to Cision\/PR Newswire, the oldest and largest press release eReleases.com","foundingDate":"1998-10-01","naics":"541820","numberOfEmployees":{"@type":"QuantitativeValue","minValue":"1","maxValue":"10"},"ownershipFundingInfo":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/author-mickie-kennedy\/"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749226908590","position":1,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749226908590","name":"How strict should I be about following AP style in my press releases?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"For press releases intended for media distribution, you should follow AP style as closely as possible. Journalists are accustomed to this style and may view inconsistencies as unprofessional. That said, it’s acceptable to have a few company-specific style preferences (such as how your company name is styled) as long as they’re applied consistently.","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227021780","position":2,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227021780","name":"What’s the most important AP style rule to follow in press releases?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"The inverted pyramid structure — placing the most important information in the first paragraph — is the most critical AP style principle for press releases.<br><br>Beyond structure, the rules journalists notice most immediately are proper formatting of dates (abbreviated months, no ordinal suffixes), numbers (spell out one through nine, figures for 10 and above), and quote attribution (past tense \"said,\" full name and title on first reference). Getting these right signals professional credibility before a journalist reads beyond the lead.","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227051926","position":3,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227051926","name":"Do journalists actually care if my press release follows AP style?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"Yes. Journalists receive dozens or hundreds of press releases daily. Those that follow AP style require less editing and signal professionalism. Many journalists will immediately notice style errors, which can detract from your message or even lead them to dismiss your release entirely.","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227114414","position":4,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227114414","name":"If I don’t have an AP Stylebook, where can I find reliable AP style information?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"While purchasing the AP Stylebook is the best option, the Purdue Online Writing Lab (OWL) offers a free summary of AP style basics. Many journalism schools and media organizations also publish <a href=\"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/ap-style-what-why-it-matters\/\">free AP style cheat sheets online<\/a>. Just ensure you’re looking at current information, as AP style rules are updated annually.","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227151232","position":5,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227151232","name":"Should I follow AP style for press releases distributed internationally?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"Yes — AP style's core principles of clarity, brevity, and consistent formatting are valuable for international audiences, but add context where needed.<br><br>For international releases, include metric equivalents in parentheses after U.S. measurements, clarify regional terms that may not translate, and spell out acronyms that are industry-standard in the U.S. but unfamiliar abroad. Monetary figures should specify the currency clearly on first reference. The dateline city still follows AP style, even for releases originating outside the United States.","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227282071","position":6,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227282071","name":"How long should an AP style press release be?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"An AP style press release should be 400 to 600 words — approximately one page.<br><br>This length follows the journalistic principle of brevity while providing enough detail for a complete story. If additional information is genuinely necessary, include it in a \"Notes to Editors\" section below the boilerplate, or link to a supporting press kit. Anything beyond 600 words is rarely read in full and is unlikely to be reprinted as written.","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227314739","position":7,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227314739","name":"Is there a specific font or formatting required for printed AP style press releases?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"AP style doesn’t specify exact font requirements, but the industry standard is to use Times New Roman or Arial at 12-point size with single spacing and left alignment. Avoid decorative fonts, colored text, or excessive formatting that might distract from your message.","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227344885","position":8,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227344885","name":"Should I include images with my AP style press release?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"While AP style doesn’t address image inclusion specifically, it’s beneficial to include relevant high-quality images with your press release. Provide these as separate attachments (not embedded) with AP style captions that identify people from left to right and include relevant context.","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227377439","position":9,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227377439","name":"How should I format quotes in an AP style press release?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"Attribute quotes to specific individuals with their full name and title on first reference (just last name on subsequent references). Use “said” rather than “says,” “stated,” or other alternatives. Place the attribution after the quote, and remember that periods and commas go inside quotation marks.","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227405537","position":10,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227405537","name":"How should I structure the headline in AP style?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"AP style headlines capitalize only the first word and proper nouns. Keep headlines concise (ideally under 80-100 characters) and focused on the key news element. Avoid clickbait, questions, or excessive punctuation.","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227483169","position":11,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227483169","name":"Should I use the Oxford comma in my press release?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"AP style generally avoids the Oxford comma (the comma before “and” or “or” in a series), but allows it when necessary for clarity. For example: “The company offers services in marketing, social media and web design” (no Oxford comma) vs. “The CEO thanked her parents, Oprah Winfrey, and Michelle Obama” (Oxford comma used to clarify that her parents are not Oprah and Michelle).","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227522206","position":12,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227522206","name":"How do I format dates in AP style?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"Abbreviate months that are six or more letters long when they appear with a specific date: Jan., Feb., Aug., Sept., Oct., Nov., Dec.<br><br>March, April, May, June, and July are never abbreviated. Do not use ordinal indicators (1st, 2nd, 3rd) with dates. When the year appears mid-sentence, set it off with commas: \"The product launched Jan. 15, 2025, and has since...\" When writing a month and year without a specific date, spell out the full month: \"The report, published in September 2024, found...\"","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227552920","position":13,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227552920","name":"How should I handle numbers in my press release?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"Spell out numbers one through nine; use figures for 10 and above.<br><br>Always use figures — regardless of the number — for ages, percentages (using the % symbol), dimensions, temperatures, speeds, monetary amounts, addresses, and any statistical data. A correct AP style sentence might read: \"The five-year-old company employs 12 people across three cities and reported $2.4 million in revenue last year.\" Note that \"5-year-old\" (an age) uses a figure, while \"three cities\" (a count below 10) is spelled out.","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227613547","position":14,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227613547","name":"How do I properly format my company’s name if it doesn’t follow AP style rules?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"AP style generally respects a company’s preferred styling of its name, even when it breaks standard rules. If your company name includes unusual capitalization (SalesForce), no spaces (LinkedIn), or special punctuation (Yahoo!), maintain that styling throughout your press release.","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227640399","position":15,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227640399","name":"Should I spell out acronyms on first reference?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"In general, spell out acronyms on first reference followed by the acronym in parentheses, then use just the acronym for subsequent references. Widely recognized acronyms like FBI, NASA, or CEO don’t need to be spelled out. Industry-specific acronyms should always be spelled out on first reference.","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227869952","position":16,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227869952","name":"How do I adapt AP style for digital press releases?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"For digital press releases, all core AP style rules still apply. Additional considerations include using SEO-friendly headlines while maintaining AP style capitalization, creating shorter paragraphs for screen readability, and properly formatting hyperlinks (use descriptive anchor text, not “click here”).","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227906233","position":17,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227906233","name":"Should hyperlinks be styled differently in an AP style press release?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"AP style doesn’t provide specific guidance on hyperlink formatting, but best practices include:<br\/>- Don’t hyperlink entire paragraphs or sentences<br\/>- Use descriptive anchor text rather than raw URLs or phrases like “click here”<br\/>- When sharing raw URLs in a press release, include the http:\/\/ or https:\/\/ prefix","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227962657","position":18,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749227962657","name":"How should I handle multimedia elements in digital press releases?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"For digital press releases, describe available multimedia elements at the end of the release rather than embedding them. For example: “High-resolution images, B-roll footage, and executive headshots are available in this digital press kit: [link].” Provide captions for all visual elements following AP style guidelines.","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228048887","position":19,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228048887","name":"How do I format product names that don’t follow standard capitalization?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"Follow the company’s official styling of product names, even when they break AP style rules (iPhone, WordPerfect). If the product name begins a sentence, either rewrite the sentence or capitalize normally: “IPhone users will notice improvements” would be incorrect; instead write “iPhone users will notice improvements” or “Users of iPhone will notice improvements.”","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228081370","position":20,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228081370","name":"Are there specific AP style guidelines for technical press releases?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"Follow the company's official styling of product names, even when they violate standard capitalization rules.<br><br>AP style respects trade names, so iPhone, WordPress, YouTube, and similar names retain their unconventional capitalization throughout the release. The one practical complication is sentence-starting: if a product name beginning with a lowercase letter would open a sentence, rewrite the sentence to avoid starting with it. Write \"Users of iPhone noticed...\" rather than \"IPhone users noticed...\" — the latter looks like a typo.","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228108488","position":21,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228108488","name":"How should financial figures be handled in AP style press releases?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"Use figures for all monetary amounts. Use the $ sign for dollars, spell out “cents” for amounts under a dollar, and use decimals for even dollar amounts only when they appear with amounts that include cents. For example: “$5,” “50 cents,” “$5.25,” and “$5 million” are all correct AP style.","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228135779","position":22,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228135779","name":"Is AP style different for crisis communication press releases?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"The core AP style rules remain the same for crisis communications, though these releases typically emphasize straightforward, factual information with less marketing language. Attribution becomes particularly important; clearly identify who is making statements about the crisis situation.","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228166519","position":23,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228166519","name":"How should I handle job titles in an AP style press release?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"Capitalize job titles when they precede a name, but lowercase when they follow a name or stand alone. For example: “Chief Executive Officer Jane Smith announced…” but “Jane Smith, chief executive officer, announced…” or “The chief executive officer announced…”","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228238810","position":24,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228238810","name":"How does eReleases ensure my press release follows AP style?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"eReleases has editors who review all press releases for AP style compliance before distribution. They can catch common errors and suggest corrections to ensure your release meets professional standards, saving you from potential embarrassment and improving your chances of media pickup.","inLanguage":"en-US"},"inLanguage":"en-US"},{"@type":"Question","@id":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228270582","position":25,"url":"https:\/\/www.ereleases.com\/press-release-sample\/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide\/#faq-question-1749228270582","name":"Will eReleases edit my press release if it doesn’t follow AP style?","answerCount":1,"acceptedAnswer":{"@type":"Answer","text":"eReleases offers professional editing services that include AP style corrections. For minor issues, their editors may make adjustments automatically. var esb_data = {"post_content":"Ask AI for a short summary of this article \r\n\r\nAP Style for Press Releases\r\nWhen your small business has news to share with the world, a press release \u2014 sometimes called a news release \u2014 is often your best communication tool. But not all press releases are created equal. The difference between one that gets picked up by journalists and one that gets ignored often comes down to a single factor: the proper usage of AP style.\r\nTable of Contents\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nThe Importance of Writing Press Releases in AP Style\r\nThe Inverted Pyramid Structure\r\nAnatomy of an AP-Style Press Release\r\nExample Press Release Template in AP Style\r\nAP Style for Digital Press Releases\r\nMobile Optimization\r\nIncorporating SEO into AP Style Press Releases\r\nFrequently Asked Questions\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nWhat Is AP Style?\r\nAP style \u2014 short for Associated Press style \u2014 is a standardized set of rules for grammar, punctuation, spelling, capitalization, and language usage developed by The Associated Press and published annually in the AP Stylebook. \r\nOriginally created to ensure consistency across wire service reporting, it has become the default writing standard for journalists, press release writers, and communications professionals across the United States and much of the world. \r\nAP style governs practical decisions like when to spell out numbers versus use figures, how to abbreviate months and state names, how to capitalize job titles, and how to attribute quotes \u2014 always with \"said\" rather than \"stated\" or \"noted.\" \r\nIts underlying philosophy is clarity and brevity: every rule exists to make copy easier to read and faster to edit on deadline. \r\nFor businesses writing press releases, following AP style is vital because it speaks the same language journalists use every day, reducing the editing burden on reporters and improving the chances that your release gets picked up and published.\r\nThe Importance of Writing Press Releases in AP Style\r\nThe Associated Press (AP) Stylebook has been the gold standard for journalistic writing since 1846. Updated annually, it provides consistent guidelines for grammar, spelling, punctuation, and language usage that journalists rely on daily. For small businesses targeting a national audience, mastering AP style isn't just about looking professional\u2014it's about speaking the same language as the media professionals you're trying to reach.\r\nWhy does this matter? Media outlets receive hundreds of press releases daily. When yours follows AP style, it demonstrates professionalism and credibility while making journalists' jobs easier. They can quickly scan your release and, if interested, even use portions of it verbatim in their coverage without extensive editing. This increased efficiency dramatically improves your chances of getting media coverage.\r\nThe Most Important AP Style Fundamental for Press Releases - The Inverted Pyramid Structure\r\nBefore diving into specific style rules, it's important to understand the structural approach that AP style embodies. The \"inverted pyramid\" structure places the most important information at the beginning of your press release, with supporting details following in descending order of importance.\r\nThis means your first paragraph should answer the five essential questions: who, what, when, where, and why. This approach ensures that even if journalists only read the first few paragraphs, they'll still capture the essential news value of your announcement.\r\nAnatomy of an AP-Style Press Release\r\nA properly formatted AP-style press release follows a specific structure that journalists immediately recognize.\r\nHeader Elements\r\nAt the top of your press release:\r\n\r\nCenter your business logo at the very top\r\nPlace \"FOR IMMEDIATE RELEASE\" in all caps on the left side (or \"EMBARGOED FOR RELEASE UNTIL [DATE AND TIME]\" if applicable)\r\nInclude contact information (name, phone, email) for your media contact\r\n\r\nHeadline and Subheadline Best Practices\r\nYour headline should be concise, attention-grabbing, and in AP style:\r\n\r\nUse title case for headlines. This means capitalizing the first word, the last word and all major words in between. \r\nAim for headlines under 80 characters for digital distribution\r\nInclude a subheadline (also called a dek) that provides additional context if necessary\r\nBalance SEO keywords with natural language that follows AP style\r\n\r\nAP Style Headline Capitalization Rules\r\nAP style uses title case for press release headlines. That means capitalizing the first word, the last word, and all major words in between \u2014 regardless of their length.\r\nWords that are not capitalized in AP style headlines (unless they are the first or last word):\r\n\r\nArticles: a, an, the\r\nShort conjunctions: and, but, or, nor, for, so, yet\r\nShort prepositions: in, on, at, by, to, up, as, of, off\r\n\r\nWords that are always capitalized, even if short:\r\n\r\nVerbs, including short ones: Is, Are, Be, Do, Go\r\nNouns and pronouns: He, She, It, We, They\r\nAdjectives and adverbs: New, Fast, Now, Well\r\n\r\n\r\nSubheadlines (also called deks) follow the same title case rules. Keep both headline and subheadline under 80 characters combined for optimal digital display. Avoid punctuation at the end of headlines unless a question mark or exclamation point is grammatically necessary \u2014 in practice, use neither.\r\nDateline Format\r\nAn AP style dateline is the city name, written in all caps, that opens the first paragraph of a press release, separated from the body text by a long dash. It tells readers where and, optionally, when the news originates. Getting the dateline right signals to journalists that the rest of your release was written with the same care.\r\n\r\nFollow these rules for every dateline:\r\n\r\nUse all caps for the city name (CHICAGO, NEW YORK, PORTLAND).\r\nAdd the state abbreviation if the city is not on the AP standalone list (PORTLAND, Ore. \u2014 not PORTLAND).\r\nSeparate the dateline from the body text with a long dash and a single space on each side: CITY \u2014 Body text begins here.\r\nFor the 30 major cities listed in the table below, no state name is needed \u2014 AP style recognizes them as unambiguous on their own.\r\nInclude the date in the dateline only when it adds meaningful context to the news value of the release.\r\n\r\nExamples:\r\nNEW YORK \u2014 Company X today announced\u2026\r\nPORTLAND, Ore. \u2014 Company Y revealed\u2026\r\nMIAMI \u2014 The organization confirmed\u2026\r\nAP Style Cities That Stand Alone\r\nThe following 29 U.S. cities and Washington (D.C.) do not require a state name in AP datelines. Any city not on this list must include the abbreviated state name.\r\n\r\nState abbreviations follow AP style \u2014 not the two-letter postal codes used by USPS. For example, Oregon is Ore., California is Calif., and Massachusetts is Mass. A full list of AP state abbreviations can be found here.\r\nLead Paragraph\r\nThe opening paragraph is critical:\r\n\r\nAnswer who, what, when, where, and why in the first paragraph\r\nKeep it under 35 words if possible\r\nLead with the news, not background information\r\nAvoid starting with phrases like \"Today, the company announced...\"\r\n\r\nBody Content Organization\r\nThe remainder of your words\r\nInclude founding date, mission, key achievements, and website URL\r\nWrite in third person\r\nUse the same boilerplate consistently across all press releases\r\n\r\nMedia Contact \"12-month,\" \"61%,\" \"38%,\" \"800,\" \"$1,200,\" \"$500,000\" all use figures as required.\r\nAttribution: \"said Maria Reyes\" \u2014 past tense, name before title, placed after the quote.\r\n\r\nSee more specific AP Style rules here.\r\nAP Style for Digital Press Releases\r\nWhile traditional AP style was developed for print, digital press releases require some adaptations:\r\nBalancing SEO Needs with AP Style Requirements\r\nStrategic keyword placement is essential for digital visibility:\r\n\r\nInclude primary keywords in headline and first paragraph\r\nUse secondary keywords throughout but avoid keyword stuffing\r\nMaintain AP style even while incorporating keywords\r\nConsider using keywords in subheadings where appropriate\r\n\r\nHyperlinking Guidelines\r\nAP style for hyperlinks:\r\n\r\nHyperlink text should make sense out of context\r\nDon't hyperlink entire paragraphs\r\nAvoid phrases like \"click here\"\r\nDon't hyperlink the company name in the boilerplate section\r\n\r\nFormatting for Email Distribution\r\nWhen sending press releases via email:\r\n\r\nInclude the release in the body of the email, not just as an attachment\r\nUse plain text or limited HTML formatting\r\nCreate a compelling subject line that includes your company name\r\nDon't use all caps or excessive punctuation in the subject line\r\n\r\nAP Style for Multimedia Elements\r\nWhen including multimedia assets:\r\n\r\nProvide captions for all images following AP style (brief, factual descriptions)\r\nInclude photo credits where appropriate\r\nDon't embed videos directly in email releases; instead, provide links\r\nOffer a downloadable press kit with high-resolution images\r\n\r\nMobile Optimization\r\nWith journalists and readers increasingly consuming content on mobile devices, ensuring your AP style press releases remain properly formatted for mobile viewing is essential:\r\nText Formatting for Mobile\r\n\r\nShorter Paragraphs: Limit paragraphs to 2-3 sentences maximum for better readability on small screens\r\nBulleted Lists: Use bulleted lists for key points, which are more mobile-friendly than dense paragraphs\r\nSubheadings: Incorporate brief subheadings to break up text and improve scannability\r\nWhite Space: Increase white space between elements to improve mobile readability\r\n\r\nTechnical Considerations\r\n\r\nResponsive Design: When distributing via email, use responsive email templates that adapt to screen size\r\nFile Size: Keep any attached images under 1MB to ensure quick loading on mobile devices\r\nPlain Text Alternative: Always include a plain text version alongside HTML for email distribution\r\nLink Formatting: Make sure hyperlinks have sufficient space around them to prevent \"fat finger\" mis-clicks\r\n\r\nMobile-Friendly Multimedia\r\n\r\nImage Dimensions: Optimize images to display properly on mobile (800px width maximum)\r\nAlt Text: Include AP style descriptions as alt text for all images\r\nVideo Links: Instead of embedding videos, provide links to mobile-friendly platforms\r\nInfographics: Create mobile-specific versions of infographics with larger text and simpler layouts\r\n\r\nTesting\r\n\r\nDevice Testing: Preview your press release on multiple devices before distribution\r\nEmail Client Testing: Check formatting across major email clients used by journalists\r\nDownload Speed: Test download time for any attachments on typical mobile connections\r\n\r\nIncorporating SEO into AP Style Press Releases\r\nOne important strategic advantage of press releases is their tendency to improve your search engine ranking (SEO.) Balancing search engine optimization with AP style requirements requires strategic integration of keywords while maintaining journalistic standards:\r\nHeadline Optimization\r\nAP Style Only:\r\nCompany launches new software platform\r\nAP Style with SEO:\r\nCompany launches new AI-powered inventory management software\r\nThe SEO version incorporates specific keywords (AI-powered, inventory management) while maintaining AP style capitalization rules.\r\nLead Paragraph Optimization\r\nAP Style Only:\r\nNEW YORK \u2014 A technology company released a new platform today aimed at helping businesses improve operations.\r\nAP Style with SEO:\r\nNEW YORK \u2014 XYZ Technology released its new cloud-based inventory management software today, designed to help small businesses reduce stockouts and overstock situations.\r\nThe SEO version naturally incorporates target keywords (cloud-based inventory management, small businesses, stockouts) while maintaining the essential AP style elements.\r\nBoilerplate Optimization\r\nAP Style Only:\r\nAbout Company X:\r\nCompany X provides software solutions for businesses. The company was founded in 2020 and is based in New York.\r\nAP Style with SEO:\r\nAbout XYZ Technology:\r\nXYZ Technology develops cloud-based inventory management software for small and medium-sized retailers. Founded in 2020 and headquartered in New York, the company helps more than 500 retailers optimize their inventory processes and reduce operational costs. Learn more at xyztechnology.com.\r\nThe SEO version naturally incorporates target keywords and phrases throughout while maintaining AP style formatting.\r\nBalancing SEO and AP Style Tips\r\n\r\nPlace primary keywords in the headline and first paragraph without forcing them\r\nUse secondary keywords in subsequent paragraphs where they fit naturally\r\nMaintain proper AP style for numbers, dates, and titles even when incorporating keywords\r\nUse industry-specific terms that journalists and searchers would both recognize\r\nAvoid keyword stuffing, which violates both SEO best practices and AP style's emphasis on clear writing\r\n\r\nThe 10 Most Common AP Style Mistakes in Press Releases\r\nEven careful writers make predictable errors when adapting AP style for press releases. Here are the ten mistakes journalists notice most \u2014 and how to fix them.\r\n1. Writing out \"percent\" instead of using the % symbol. \r\nAP style updated its guidance on this in 2019. Use the % symbol in all cases except at the start of a sentence. Correct: \"Sales increased 14% year over year.\" Incorrect: \"Sales increased 14 percent year over year.\"\r\n2. Using ordinal indicators with dates.\r\nAP style never uses 1st, 2nd, 3rd, or 4th with dates. Write \"The event takes place Jan. 15\" \u2014 not \"January 15th.\" The month abbreviation rules also catch many writers off guard: only Jan., Feb., Aug., Sept., Oct., Nov., and Dec. are abbreviated. March, April, May, June, and July are always spelled out.\r\n3. Capitalizing job titles that follow a name.\r\nCapitalize titles only when they directly precede a full name: \"Chief Executive Officer Jane Smith announced...\" But lowercase when the title follows the name or stands alone: \"Jane Smith, chief executive officer, announced...\" or \"The chief executive officer said...\" This rule trips up nearly every first-time press release writer.\r\n4. Using \"says\" instead of \"said\" in quote attribution.\r\nAP style uses past tense for attribution. Write \"said Jane Smith\" \u2014 not \"says Jane Smith,\" \"stated,\" \"noted,\" \"explained,\" or \"exclaimed.\" The word \"said\" is invisible to readers in a way that fancier attribution verbs are not, which is exactly why AP style insists on it.\r\n5. Spelling out numbers 10 and above.\r\nSpell out one through nine; use figures for 10 and above. However, always use figures \u2014 regardless of size \u2014 for ages, percentages, dimensions, temperatures, speeds, monetary amounts, and addresses. A sentence like \"The five-year-old company employs 12 people across three offices\" is fully correct AP style.\r\n6. Using the Oxford comma. \r\nAP style omits the serial comma (the comma before \"and\" or \"or\" in a list) unless it is needed to prevent confusion. Write \"The company offers services in marketing, social media and web design\" \u2014 not \"marketing, social media, and web design.\" The exception: use the Oxford comma when omitting it creates genuine ambiguity.\r\n7. Abbreviating state names incorrectly. \r\nAP uses its own state abbreviations \u2014 not the two-letter USPS postal codes. California is Calif., not CA. Texas is Texas (never abbreviated). Florida is Fla., not FL. These are distinct from postal codes, and journalists will notice the difference immediately.\r\n8. Starting the lead with the company name.\r\nA dateline-style press release should open with the news, not the company. \"CHICAGO \u2014 Acme Corp. today announced...\" buries the news behind the brand. Stronger: \"CHICAGO \u2014 A new cloud accounting tool designed for sole proprietors launches today...\" Lead with what happened; attribute it in the second or third sentence.\r\n9. Writing the boilerplate in first person.\r\nThe \"About [Company]\" section should always be written in the third person. Avoid \"We are a digital marketing agency.\" Write instead: \"Acme is a digital marketing agency founded in 2012.\" Journalists may lift boilerplate language directly, and first-person language sounds odd in a news context.\r\n10. Forgetting the end mark. \r\nEvery press release ends with ### centered on its own line. This is not decorative \u2014 it is a signal to journalists (and wire services) that the release is complete and that nothing was cut off in transmission. Omitting it looks amateurish to anyone who has read a press release before.\r\nPress Release Best Practices for \r\nAP style rules are the foundation of an effective press release, but following them alone does not guarantee coverage. These best practices reflect both journalistic standards and how press releases are distributed and discovered in .\r\nBefore You Write\r\n\r\nDetermine whether your news is genuinely newsworthy. Ask: Would a journalist covering your industry care about this if they heard about it from someone other than your company? If the honest answer is no, consider whether a blog post or social update is a better fit than a press release.\r\nIdentify your target audience before writing. A release for a trade publication requires different framing than one aimed at the general business press.\r\nChoose one angle and build the release around it. Trying to announce multiple things in one release dilutes both the news value and the SEO focus.\r\n\r\nWriting the Release\r\n\r\nLead with the most important information. Journalists scan releases in seconds \u2014 if the news is not in the first sentence, many will not find it.\r\nWrite the headline last. Once the body is complete, you will have a clearer sense of the single strongest angle.\r\nUse one quote per release, with a maximum of two. Quotes should add perspective that the body copy cannot \u2014 not restate facts that are already there.\r\nKeep the release to 400-600 words. Longer releases are read less frequently and are harder to repurpose for news coverage.\r\nUse active voice. Passive constructions slow down the read and are more likely to be rewritten or cut by a journalist on deadline.\r\n\r\nDistribution and Timing\r\n\r\nSend press releases on Tuesday, Wednesday, or Thursday, between 10 a.m. and 2 p.m. in the target journalist's time zone. Mondays and Fridays see lower pickup rates, and releases sent after 3 p.m. are more likely to be missed before the end of the day.\r\nEmbargo releases strategically. If you need to give journalists lead time, an embargo can be appropriate, but set a specific embargo lift time and honor it exactly.\r\nFollow up once, not repeatedly. A single follow-up email 48 to 72 hours after distribution is professional; multiple follow-ups damage the relationship.\r\n\r\nSEO and Digital Visibility\r\n\r\nPlace your primary keyword naturally in the headline and the first paragraph. Do not force it. If it reads awkwardly, rewrite the sentence.\r\nInclude at least one internal link to a related resource on your website. This supports both SEO and the journalist's ability to learn more about your company.\r\nAdd alt text to any images distributed with the release. Image search drives meaningful referral traffic, and AP style image captions make good alt text with minor adaptation.\r\nUpdate evergreen press release content \u2014 like this page \u2014 when guidance changes. Stale examples and outdated rules reduce credibility with journalists and search engines alike.\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nGeneral Questions\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nHow strict should I be about following AP style in my press releases? For press releases intended for media distribution, you should follow AP style as closely as possible. Journalists are accustomed to this style and may view inconsistencies as unprofessional. That said, it\u2019s acceptable to have a few company-specific style preferences (such as how your company name is styled) as long as they\u2019re applied consistently.  What\u2019s the most important AP style rule to follow in press releases? The inverted pyramid structure \u2014 placing the most important information in the first paragraph \u2014 is the most critical AP style principle for press releases.Beyond structure, the rules journalists notice most immediately are proper formatting of dates (abbreviated months, no ordinal suffixes), numbers (spell out one through nine, figures for 10 and above), and quote attribution (past tense \"said,\" full name and title on first reference). Getting these right signals professional credibility before a journalist reads beyond the lead.  Do journalists actually care if my press release follows AP style? Yes. Journalists receive dozens or hundreds of press releases daily. Those that follow AP style require less editing and signal professionalism. Many journalists will immediately notice style errors, which can detract from your message or even lead them to dismiss your release entirely.  If I don\u2019t have an AP Stylebook, where can I find reliable AP style information? While purchasing the AP Stylebook is the best option, the Purdue Online Writing Lab (OWL) offers a free summary of AP style basics. Many journalism schools and media organizations also publish\u00a0free AP style cheat sheets online. Just ensure you\u2019re looking at current information, as AP style rules are updated annually.  Should I follow AP style for press releases distributed internationally? Yes \u2014 AP style's core principles of clarity, brevity, and consistent formatting are valuable for international audiences, but add context where needed.For international releases, include metric equivalents in parentheses after U.S. measurements, clarify regional terms that may not translate, and spell out acronyms that are industry-standard in the U.S. but unfamiliar abroad. Monetary figures should specify the currency clearly on first reference. The dateline city still follows AP style, even for releases originating outside the United States.  \r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nFormatting and Structure\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nHow long should an AP style press release be? An AP style press release should be 400 to 600 words \u2014 approximately one page.This length follows the journalistic principle of brevity while providing enough detail for a complete story. If additional information is genuinely necessary, include it in a \"Notes to Editors\" section below the boilerplate, or link to a supporting press kit. Anything beyond 600 words is rarely read in full and is unlikely to be reprinted as written.  Is there a specific font or formatting required for printed AP style press releases? AP style doesn\u2019t specify exact font requirements, but the industry standard is to use Times New Roman or Arial at 12-point size with single spacing and left alignment. Avoid decorative fonts, colored text, or excessive formatting that might distract from your message.  Should I include images with my AP style press release? While AP style doesn\u2019t address image inclusion specifically, it\u2019s beneficial to include relevant high-quality images with your press release. Provide these as separate attachments (not embedded) with AP style captions that identify people from left to right and include relevant context.  How should I format quotes in an AP style press release? Attribute quotes to specific individuals with their full name and title on first reference (just last name on subsequent references). Use \u201csaid\u201d rather than \u201csays,\u201d \u201cstated,\u201d or other alternatives. Place the attribution after the quote, and remember that periods and commas go inside quotation marks.  How should I structure the headline in AP style? AP style headlines capitalize only the first word and proper nouns. Keep headlines concise (ideally under 80-100 characters) and focused on the key news element. Avoid clickbait, questions, or excessive punctuation.  \r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nCommon AP Style Questions for Press Releases\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nShould I use the Oxford comma in my press release? AP style generally avoids the Oxford comma (the comma before \u201cand\u201d or \u201cor\u201d in a series), but allows it when necessary for clarity. For example: \u201cThe company offers services in marketing, social media and web design\u201d (no Oxford comma) vs. \u201cThe CEO thanked her parents, Oprah Winfrey, and Michelle Obama\u201d (Oxford comma used to clarify that her parents are not Oprah and Michelle).  How do I format dates in AP style? Abbreviate months that are six or more letters long when they appear with a specific date: Jan., Feb., Aug., Sept., Oct., Nov., Dec.March, April, May, June, and July are never abbreviated. Do not use ordinal indicators (1st, 2nd, 3rd) with dates. When the year appears mid-sentence, set it off with commas: \"The product launched Jan. 15, 2025, and has since...\" When writing a month and year without a specific date, spell out the full month: \"The report, published in September 2024, found...\"  How should I handle numbers in my press release? Spell out numbers one through nine; use figures for 10 and above.Always use figures \u2014 regardless of the number \u2014 for ages, percentages (using the % symbol), dimensions, temperatures, speeds, monetary amounts, addresses, and any statistical data. A correct AP style sentence might read: \"The five-year-old company employs 12 people across three cities and reported $2.4 million in revenue last year.\" Note that \"5-year-old\" (an age) uses a figure, while \"three cities\" (a count below 10) is spelled out.  How do I properly format my company\u2019s name if it doesn\u2019t follow AP style rules? AP style generally respects a company\u2019s preferred styling of its name, even when it breaks standard rules. If your company name includes unusual capitalization (SalesForce), no spaces (LinkedIn), or special punctuation (Yahoo!), maintain that styling throughout your press release.  Should I spell out acronyms on first reference? In general, spell out acronyms on first reference followed by the acronym in parentheses, then use just the acronym for subsequent references. Widely recognized acronyms like FBI, NASA, or CEO don\u2019t need to be spelled out. Industry-specific acronyms should always be spelled out on first reference.  \r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nDigital Considerations\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nHow do I adapt AP style for digital press releases? For digital press releases, all core AP style rules still apply. Additional considerations include using SEO-friendly headlines while maintaining AP style capitalization, creating shorter paragraphs for screen readability, and properly formatting hyperlinks (use descriptive anchor text, not \u201cclick here\u201d).  Should hyperlinks be styled differently in an AP style press release? AP style doesn\u2019t provide specific guidance on hyperlink formatting, but best practices include:- Don\u2019t hyperlink entire paragraphs or sentences- Use descriptive anchor text rather than raw URLs or phrases like \u201cclick here\u201d- When sharing raw URLs in a press release, include the http:// or https:// prefix  How should I handle multimedia elements in digital press releases? For digital press releases, describe available multimedia elements at the end of the release rather than embedding them. For example: \u201cHigh-resolution images, B-roll footage, and executive headshots are available in this digital press kit: [link].\u201d Provide captions for all visual elements following AP style guidelines.  \r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nIndustry-Specific Questions\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nHow do I format product names that don\u2019t follow standard capitalization? Follow the company\u2019s official styling of product names, even when they break AP style rules (iPhone, WordPerfect). If the product name begins a sentence, either rewrite the sentence or capitalize normally: \u201cIPhone users will notice improvements\u201d would be incorrect; instead write \u201ciPhone users will notice improvements\u201d or \u201cUsers of iPhone will notice improvements.\u201d  Are there specific AP style guidelines for technical press releases? Follow the company's official styling of product names, even when they violate standard capitalization rules.AP style respects trade names, so iPhone, WordPress, YouTube, and similar names retain their unconventional capitalization throughout the release. The one practical complication is sentence-starting: if a product name beginning with a lowercase letter would open a sentence, rewrite the sentence to avoid starting with it. Write \"Users of iPhone noticed...\" rather than \"IPhone users noticed...\" \u2014 the latter looks like a typo.  How should financial figures be handled in AP style press releases? Use figures for all monetary amounts. Use the $ sign for dollars, spell out \u201ccents\u201d for amounts under a dollar, and use decimals for even dollar amounts only when they appear with amounts that include cents. For example: \u201c$5,\u201d \u201c50 cents,\u201d \u201c$5.25,\u201d and \u201c$5 million\u201d are all correct AP style.  Is AP style different for crisis communication press releases? The core AP style rules remain the same for crisis communications, though these releases typically emphasize straightforward, factual information with less marketing language. Attribution becomes particularly important; clearly identify who is making statements about the crisis situation.  How should I handle job titles in an AP style press release? Capitalize job titles when they precede a name, but lowercase when they follow a name or stand alone. For example: \u201cChief Executive Officer Jane Smith announced\u2026\u201d but \u201cJane Smith, chief executive officer, announced\u2026\u201d or \u201cThe chief executive officer announced\u2026\u201d  \r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nWorking with eReleases\r\n\r\n\r\n\r\nHow does eReleases ensure my press release follows AP style? eReleases has editors who review all press releases for AP style compliance before distribution. They can catch common errors and suggest corrections to ensure your release meets professional standards, saving you from potential embarrassment and improving your chances of media pickup.  Will eReleases edit my press release if it doesn\u2019t follow AP style? eReleases offers professional editing services that include AP style corrections. For minor issues, their editors may make adjustments automatically. For more significant revisions, they\u2019ll work with you to bring your release into compliance with AP style guidelines while preserving your key messages.  Can eReleases help create an AP style press release from scratch? Yes,\u00a0eReleases offers press release writing services\u00a0from experienced writers who are well-versed in AP style. Their team can craft professional press releases that adhere to all relevant guidelines while effectively communicating your news to target media.  How important is AP style for press release distribution success? Very important. eReleases distributes to journalists who expect professionally formatted content. Press releases that follow AP style demonstrate media savvy and respect for journalists\u2019 time, significantly increasing the likelihood of coverage. eReleases\u2019 data shows that properly formatted releases typically receive more media attention than those with style inconsistencies.  Does eReleases offer resources to help me learn AP style? eReleases provides clients with style guides and tips for creating effective press releases. Their blog also features articles about AP style best practices. Additionally, their editing team can provide feedback that helps you improve your understanding of AP style for future releases.  \r\n\r\n\r\nProfessional Press Release Distribution with eReleases\r\nCreating an AP-style press release is only half the battle\u2014the other half is getting it into the right hands.\r\nWhy Professional Distribution Matters\r\nEven the most perfectly formatted press release won't generate coverage if it doesn't reach the right journalists. eReleases solves this problem by:\r\n\r\nTargeting industry-specific journalists who cover your niche\r\nProviding access to premium distribution networks typically only available to large corporations\r\nOffering placement on major news sites and industry publications\r\nIncluding guaranteed distribution through PR Newswire, reaching thousands of newsrooms\r\n\r\nHow eReleases Maximizes Your AP-Style Press Release\r\neReleases ensures your carefully crafted press release achieves maximum impact:\r\n\r\nExpert editors review your release to confirm AP style compliance\r\nStrategic distribution to relevant journalists based on your industry and target market\r\nDetailed reports showing who received and engaged with your release\r\nSyndication across online news platforms to boost SEO and visibility\r\n\r\nSpecial Offer for Small Businesses\r\nGet your AP-style press release in front of the right journalists with eReleases' small business package:\r\n\r\nProfessional distribution to targeted media lists\r\nAP style review and editing assistance\r\nGuaranteed placement on major news sites\r\nComprehensive reporting on your release's performance\r\n\r\nTake the Next Step\r\nVisit eReleases.com today to ensure your AP-style press release gets maximum visibility with the journalists who matter to your business. With eReleases, your small business can achieve the media coverage it deserves."};
