Copilot</button></div></div></em></p></pre> <p><img alt="" width="201" height="201" sizes="(max-width: 201px) 100vw, 201px" nitro-lazy-srcset="https://cdn-eaebb.nitrocdn.com/QvzDcOKmPZNTGhotvWgVpUsyPfCTqwim/assets/images/optimized/rev-e978aec/media.ereleases.com/uploads/2025/03/AP-style-for-press-releases-300x300.png 300w, https://cdn-eaebb.nitrocdn.com/QvzDcOKmPZNTGhotvWgVpUsyPfCTqwim/assets/images/optimized/rev-e978aec/media.ereleases.com/uploads/2025/03/AP-style-for-press-releases-100x100.png 100w, https://cdn-eaebb.nitrocdn.com/QvzDcOKmPZNTGhotvWgVpUsyPfCTqwim/assets/images/optimized/rev-e978aec/media.ereleases.com/uploads/2025/03/AP-style-for-press-releases-768x768.png 768w, https://cdn-eaebb.nitrocdn.com/QvzDcOKmPZNTGhotvWgVpUsyPfCTqwim/assets/images/optimized/rev-e978aec/media.ereleases.com/uploads/2025/03/AP-style-for-press-releases-140x140.png 140w, https://cdn-eaebb.nitrocdn.com/QvzDcOKmPZNTGhotvWgVpUsyPfCTqwim/assets/images/optimized/rev-e978aec/media.ereleases.com/uploads/2025/03/AP-style-for-press-releases-200x200.png 200w, https://cdn-eaebb.nitrocdn.com/QvzDcOKmPZNTGhotvWgVpUsyPfCTqwim/assets/images/optimized/rev-e978aec/media.ereleases.com/uploads/2025/03/AP-style-for-press-releases.png 1024w" nitro-lazy-src="https://cdn-eaebb.nitrocdn.com/QvzDcOKmPZNTGhotvWgVpUsyPfCTqwim/assets/images/optimized/rev-e978aec/media.ereleases.com/uploads/2025/03/AP-style-for-press-releases-300x300.png" class="wp-image-47316 alignright nitro-lazy" decoding="async" nitro-lazy-empty id="MTY5Mjo3NjQ=-1" data-nitro-empty-id="MTY5Mjo3NjQ=-1" src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB2aWV3Qm94PSIwIDAgMzAwIDMwMCIgd2lkdGg9IjMwMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIzMDAiIHhtbG5zPSJodHRwOi8vd3d3LnczLm9yZy8yMDAwL3N2ZyI+PC9zdmc+" /></p> <h2>AP Style for Press Releases</h2> <p>When your small business has news to share with the world, a press release — sometimes called a news release — is often your best communication tool. But not all press releases are created equal. The difference between one that gets picked up by journalists and one that gets ignored often comes down to a single factor: the proper usage of AP style.</p> <p></p><p style="font-weight:bold;margin-bottom:10px">Table of Contents</p> <div style="width:950px"> <div style="text-align:center"> <table style="background:#ecf8ff;font-weight:normal;margin-bottom:10px"> <tbody> <tr> <td style="text-align:left;padding:10px"> <div class="row"> <ul> <li><a href="#importance">The Importance of Writing Press Releases in AP Style</a></li> <li><a href="#inverted">The Inverted Pyramid Structure</a></li> <li><a href="#anatomy">Anatomy of an AP-Style Press Release</a></li> <li><a href="#template">Example Press Release Template in AP Style</a></li> <li><a href="#digital">AP Style for Digital Press Releases</a></li> <li><a href="#mobile">Mobile Optimization</a></li> <li><a href="#seo">Incorporating SEO into AP Style Press Releases</a></li> <li><a href="#faq">Frequently Asked Questions</a></li> </ul> </div> </td> </tr> </tbody> </table> </div> </div> <h2>What Is AP Style?</h2> <p>AP style — short for Associated Press style — is a standardized set of rules for grammar, punctuation, spelling, capitalization, and language usage developed by The Associated Press and published annually in the AP Stylebook. </p> <p>Originally created to ensure consistency across wire service reporting, it has become the default writing standard for journalists, press release writers, and communications professionals across the United States and much of the world. </p> <p>AP style governs practical decisions like when to spell out numbers versus use figures, how to abbreviate months and state names, how to capitalize job titles, and how to attribute quotes — always with &#8220;said&#8221; rather than &#8220;stated&#8221; or &#8220;noted.&#8221; </p> <p>Its underlying philosophy is clarity and brevity: every rule exists to make copy easier to read and faster to edit on deadline. </p> <p>For businesses writing press releases, following AP style is vital because it speaks the same language journalists use every day, reducing the editing burden on reporters and improving the chances that your release gets picked up and published.</p> <h2 id="importance">The Importance of Writing Press Releases in AP Style</h2> <p>The Associated Press (AP) Stylebook has been the gold standard for journalistic writing since 1846. Updated annually, it provides consistent guidelines for grammar, spelling, punctuation, and language usage that journalists rely on daily. For small businesses targeting a national audience, mastering AP style isn&#8217;t just about looking professional—it&#8217;s about speaking the same language as the media professionals you&#8217;re trying to reach.</p> <p>Why does this matter? Media outlets receive hundreds of press releases daily. When yours follows AP style, it demonstrates professionalism and credibility while making journalists&#8217; jobs easier. They can quickly scan your release and, if interested, even use portions of it verbatim in their coverage without extensive editing. This increased efficiency dramatically improves your chances of getting media coverage.</p> <h2 id="inverted">The Most Important AP Style Fundamental for Press Releases &#8211; The Inverted Pyramid Structure</h2> <p>Before diving into specific style rules, it&#8217;s important to understand the structural approach that AP style embodies. The &#8220;inverted pyramid&#8221; structure places the most important information at the beginning of your press release, with supporting details following in descending order of importance.</p> <p>This means your first paragraph should answer the five essential questions: who, what, when, where, and why. This approach ensures that even if journalists only read the first few paragraphs, they&#8217;ll still capture the essential news value of your announcement.</p> <h2 id="anatomy">Anatomy of an AP-Style Press Release</h2> <p>A properly formatted AP-style press release follows a specific structure that journalists immediately recognize.</p> <h3>Header Elements</h3> <p>At the top of your press release:</p> <ul> <li>Center your business logo at the very top</li> <li>Place &#8220;FOR IMMEDIATE RELEASE&#8221; in all caps on the left side (or &#8220;EMBARGOED FOR RELEASE UNTIL [DATE AND TIME]&#8221; if applicable)</li> <li>Include contact information (name, phone, email) for your media contact</li> </ul> <h3>Headline and Subheadline Best Practices</h3> <p>Your headline should be concise, attention-grabbing, and in AP style:</p> <ul> <li>Use title case for headlines. This means capitalizing the first word, the last word and all major words in between. </li> <li>Aim for headlines under 80 characters for digital distribution</li> <li>Include a subheadline (also called a dek) that provides additional context if necessary</li> <li>Balance SEO keywords with natural language that follows AP style</li> </ul> <h3>AP Style Headline Capitalization Rules</h3> <p>AP style uses title case for press release headlines. That means capitalizing the first word, the last word, and all major words in between — regardless of their length.</p> <p>Words that are <strong>not</strong> capitalized in AP style headlines (unless they are the first or last word):</p> <ul> <li>Articles: a, an, the</li> <li>Short conjunctions: and, but, or, nor, for, so, yet</li> <li>Short prepositions: in, on, at, by, to, up, as, of, off</li> </ul> <p>Words that are <strong>always</strong> capitalized, even if short:</p> <ul> <li>Verbs, including short ones: Is, Are, Be, Do, Go</li> <li>Nouns and pronouns: He, She, It, We, They</li> <li>Adjectives and adverbs: New, Fast, Now, Well</li> </ul> <p> </p><table id="tablepress-54" class="tablepress tablepress-id-54"> <thead> <tr class="row-1"> <th class="column-1">Incorrect capitalization</th><th class="column-2">Correct AP style capitalization</th> </tr> </thead> <tbody> <tr class="row-2"> <td class="column-1">Company Launches a New Tool for small businesses</td><td class="column-2">Company Launches a New Tool for Small Businesses</td> </tr> <tr class="row-3"> <td class="column-1">How to write a press release in ap style</td><td class="column-2">How to Write a Press Release in AP Style</td> </tr> <tr class="row-4"> <td class="column-1">CEO named to Inc. list of fastest-growing companies</td><td class="column-2">CEO Named to Inc. List of Fastest-Growing Companies</td> </tr> <tr class="row-5"> <td class="column-1">New software Is now available to download</td><td class="column-2">New Software Is Now Available to Download</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <br /> Subheadlines (also called deks) follow the same title case rules. Keep both headline and subheadline under 80 characters combined for optimal digital display. Avoid punctuation at the end of headlines unless a question mark or exclamation point is grammatically necessary — in practice, use neither.<p></p> <h3>Dateline Format</h3> <p>An AP style dateline is the city name, written in all caps, that opens the first paragraph of a press release, separated from the body text by a long dash. It tells readers where and, optionally, when the news originates. Getting the dateline right signals to journalists that the rest of your release was written with the same care. </p> <p>Follow these rules for every dateline:</p> <ul> <li>Use all caps for the city name (CHICAGO, NEW YORK, PORTLAND).</li> <li>Add the state abbreviation if the city is not on the AP standalone list (PORTLAND, Ore. — not PORTLAND).</li> <li>Separate the dateline from the body text with a long dash and a single space on each side: CITY — Body text begins here.</li> <li>For the 30 major cities listed in the table below, no state name is needed — AP style recognizes them as unambiguous on their own.</li> <li>Include the date in the dateline only when it adds meaningful context to the news value of the release.</li> </ul> <p>Examples:</p> <p><em>NEW YORK — Company X today announced…</em></p> <p><em>PORTLAND, Ore. — Company Y revealed…</em></p> <p><em>MIAMI — The organization confirmed…</em></p> <h3>AP Style Cities That Stand Alone</h3> <p>The following 29 U.S. cities and Washington (D.C.) do not require a state name in AP datelines. Any city not on this list must include the abbreviated state name.</p> <table id="tablepress-55" class="tablepress tablepress-id-55"> <thead> <tr class="row-1"> <th colspan="2" class="column-1">Cities that stand alone (no state required)</th> </tr> </thead> <tbody> <tr class="row-2"> <td class="column-1">Atlanta</td><td class="column-2">Minneapolis</td> </tr> <tr class="row-3"> <td class="column-1">Boston</td><td class="column-2">New Orleans</td> </tr> <tr class="row-4"> <td class="column-1">Chicago</td><td class="column-2">New York</td> </tr> <tr class="row-5"> <td class="column-1">Cincinnati</td><td class="column-2">Oklahoma City</td> </tr> <tr class="row-6"> <td class="column-1">Cleveland</td><td class="column-2">Philadelphia</td> </tr> <tr class="row-7"> <td class="column-1">Dallas</td><td class="column-2">Phoenix</td> </tr> <tr class="row-8"> <td class="column-1">Denver</td><td class="column-2">Pittsburgh</td> </tr> <tr class="row-9"> <td class="column-1">Detroit</td><td class="column-2">Portland</td> </tr> <tr class="row-10"> <td class="column-1">Honolulu</td><td class="column-2">Salt Lake City</td> </tr> <tr class="row-11"> <td class="column-1">Houston</td><td class="column-2">San Antonio</td> </tr> <tr class="row-12"> <td class="column-1">Indianapolis</td><td class="column-2">San Francisco</td> </tr> <tr class="row-13"> <td class="column-1">Las Vegas</td><td class="column-2">Seattle</td> </tr> <tr class="row-14"> <td class="column-1">Los Angeles</td><td class="column-2">St. Louis</td> </tr> <tr class="row-15"> <td class="column-1">Miami</td><td class="column-2">Washington</td> </tr> <tr class="row-16"> <td class="column-1">Milwaukee</td><td class="column-2"></td> </tr> </tbody> </table>  <p>State abbreviations follow AP style — not the two-letter postal codes used by USPS. For example, Oregon is Ore., California is Calif., and Massachusetts is Mass. <a href="https://www.ereleases.com/pr-fuel/how-to-format-a-press-release-dateline-in-different-styles/" data-wpel-link="internal">A full list of AP state abbreviations can be found here.</a></p> <h3>Lead Paragraph</h3> <p>The opening paragraph is critical:</p> <ul> <li>Answer who, what, when, where, and why in the first paragraph</li> <li>Keep it under 35 words if possible</li> <li>Lead with the news, not background information</li> <li>Avoid starting with phrases like &#8220;Today, the company announced&#8230;&#8221;</li> </ul> <h3>Body Content Organization</h3> <p>The remainder of your press release should:</p> <ul> <li>Follow the inverted pyramid structure (most important to least important)</li> <li>Keep paragraphs short (2-3 sentences)</li> <li>Use transition words between paragraphs for logical flow</li> <li>Include 400-500 words total (one page)</li> </ul> <h3>Quote Formatting and Attribution</h3> <p>Quotes add credibility and personality:</p> <ul> <li>Always attribute quotes to specific individuals with their full names and titles on first reference</li> <li>Use &#8220;said&#8221; rather than &#8220;says,&#8221; &#8220;stated,&#8221; or other alternatives</li> <li>Put the attribution after the quote: &#8220;We&#8217;re excited about this development,&#8221; said Jane Smith, CEO of Company X.</li> <li>Use only one attribution per quote</li> </ul> <h3>Boilerplate Section</h3> <p>The &#8220;About&#8221; section appears at the end:</p> <ul> <li>Title it &#8220;About [Company Name]&#8221;</li> <li>Keep it under 100 words</li> <li>Include founding date, mission, key achievements, and website URL</li> <li>Write in third person</li> <li>Use the same boilerplate consistently across all press releases</li> </ul> <h3>Media Contact Information</h3> <p>At the very bottom, include:</p> <ul> <li>&#8220;Media Contact:&#8221; followed by the contact person&#8217;s name</li> <li>Title of contact person</li> <li>Phone number with area code</li> <li>Email address</li> <li>Company website</li> </ul> <h3>End Mark</h3> <p>Traditionally, press releases end with ###, -END- or -30- centered on the page.</p> <p>This symbol tells journalists they&#8217;ve reached the end of the release.</p> <h2 id="template">Example Press Release Template in AP Style</h2> <p>Below is an annotated example of a properly formatted AP style press release:</p> <div style="width:950px"> <div style="text-align:center"> <table style="background:#ecf8ff;font-weight:normal;margin:15px 0"> <tbody> <tr> <td style="text-align:left;padding:25px"> <div class="row"> <p>[COMPANY LOGO CENTERED HERE]</p> <p>FOR IMMEDIATE RELEASE</p> <p>Example Company Launches New Service to Help Small Businesses Grow</p> <p>CHICAGO — Example Company today announced the launch of its new ExampleGrow service, designed to help small businesses increase their online presence and customer engagement.</p> <p>The service, which becomes available Sept. 15, combines digital marketing tools with personalized coaching for businesses with fewer than 50 employees.</p> <p>&#8220;Small businesses often struggle to compete with larger corporations when it comes to digital marketing,&#8221; said John Doe, CEO of Example Company. &#8220;ExampleGrow levels the playing field by providing affordable access to the same tools and expertise.&#8221;</p> <p>The platform offers three key features:<br /> • Automated social media scheduling<br /> • Search engine optimization tools<br /> • Monthly strategy sessions with digital marketing experts</p> <p>A recent study by Example Research found that 72 percent of small businesses saw revenue decline during the pandemic, with 45 percent citing inadequate online presence as a primary factor.</p> <p>ExampleGrow subscriptions start at $99 per month with no long-term commitment required.</p> <p>Founded in 2015, Example Company has helped more than 5,000 small businesses improve their digital marketing strategies. The company has offices in Chicago, Atlanta and Denver.</p> <p>For more information about ExampleGrow, visit examplecompany.com/examplegrow.</p> <p style="text-align:center">###</p> <p>About Example Company:<br /> Example Company provides digital marketing solutions for small businesses across North America. The company&#8217;s suite of tools and services helps entrepreneurs compete effectively in the digital marketplace. Example Company was named to Inc. Magazine&#8217;s list of fastest-growing companies in 2023 and employs 75 people across three locations. Learn more at examplecompany.com.</p> <p>Media Contact:<br /> Jane Smith<br /> Media Relations Manager<br /> Example Company<br /> 555-123-4567<br /> <a href="/cdn-cgi/l/email-protection" class="__cf_email__" data-cfemail="3258415f5b465a72574a535f425e57515d5f42535c4b1c515d5f">[email&#160;protected]</a></p> </div> </td> </tr> </tbody> </table> </div> </div> <p><strong>Key elements to note:</strong></p> <ul> <li>Headline uses AP style capitalization (capitalizing the first word, the last word and all major words in between)</li> <li>Chicago doesn&#8217;t need state name in dateline (one of the 30 cities that stand alone)</li> <li>Numbers follow AP style (spelled out &#8220;three&#8221; but uses figures for &#8220;50,&#8221; &#8220;72 percent,&#8221; etc.)</li> <li>Months abbreviated properly (Sept.)</li> <li>Proper attribution for quote (person&#8217;s full name and title on first reference, &#8220;said&#8221; as the attribution verb)</li> <li>Boilerplate section kept to under 100 words</li> <li>Uses ### to mark the end of the press release</li> </ul> <h3>AP Style Format Example: Product Launch</h3> <p>The example below shows AP style applied to a product launch announcement. Compare it with the service launch example above to see how the same structural rules apply across different announcement types.</p> <div style="width:950px"> <div style="text-align:center"> <table style="background:#ecf8ff;font-weight:normal;margin-bottom:10px;margin-top:20px"> <tbody> <tr> <td style="text-align:left;padding:0 30px 30px 30px"> <div class="row"> <p></p> <p>[COMPANY LOGO CENTERED HERE]</p> <p>FOR IMMEDIATE RELEASE</p> <p><strong>Horizon Labs Releases ClearMetrics 2.0, an Analytics Platform Built for Nonprofit Organizations</strong></p> <p>SAN FRANCISCO — Horizon Labs today released ClearMetrics 2.0, a data analytics platform designed specifically for nonprofit organizations managing donor engagement and program outcomes.</p> <p>The platform, available beginning Oct. 1, replaces spreadsheet-based reporting for organizations with fewer than 200 staff and integrates directly with the five most widely used donor management systems.</p> <p>&#8220;Nonprofits are held to the same data accountability standards as corporations, but rarely have the same resources,&#8221; said Maria Reyes, chief executive officer of Horizon Labs. &#8220;ClearMetrics 2.0 closes that gap without requiring a dedicated data team.&#8221;</p> <p>ClearMetrics 2.0 includes three core modules:</p> <ul> <p>&#8211; Donor retention analytics with 12-month trend visualization</p> <p>&#8211; Program outcome reporting aligned with IRS Form 990 categories</p> <p>&#8211; Automated board report generation with export to PDF and Google Slides</p></ul> <p>A 2024 survey by the Nonprofit Technology Network found that 61% of organizations with annual budgets under $5 million rely on manual data processes for donor reporting, with 38% citing data quality as their top operational challenge.</p> <p>Annual subscriptions start at $1,200 with academic pricing available for organizations with budgets under $500,000.</p> <p>Horizon Labs was founded in 2018 and serves more than 800 nonprofit clients across North America. For more information, visit horizonlabs.io/clearmetrics.</p> <p><strong>About Horizon Labs</strong>: Horizon Labs builds data and analytics software for mission-driven organizations. Founded in 2018 and headquartered in San Francisco, the company serves nonprofit clients in education, housing, and health services. Horizon Labs was named to the Chronicle of Philanthropy&#8217;s Tech to Watch list in 2023. Learn more at <a href="http://horizonlabs.io" data-wpel-link="external" rel="external noopener">horizonlabs.io</a>.</p> <p><strong>Media Contact</strong>: David Park Communications Director, Horizon Labs 415-555-0192 <a href="/cdn-cgi/l/email-protection" class="__cf_email__" data-cfemail="354547504646755d5a475c4f5a5b595457461b5c5a">[email&#160;protected]</a></p> <p style="text-align:center">### </p></div> </td> </tr> </tbody> </table> </div> </div> <p><strong>Key elements to note in this example:</strong></p> <ul> <li><strong>Dateline</strong>: San Francisco stands alone — no state name needed (it is on the AP standalone cities list above)</li> <li><strong>Date format</strong>: &#8220;Oct. 1&#8221; — AP abbreviates October as Oct. when it appears with a specific date.</li> <li><strong>Percentage</strong>: &#8220;61%&#8221; — not &#8220;61 percent&#8221; — per updated AP style guidance.</li> <li><strong>Job title</strong>: &#8220;chief executive officer&#8221; is lowercase because it follows the name: &#8220;said Maria Reyes, chief executive officer.&#8221;</li> <li><strong>Numbers</strong>: &#8220;three core modules&#8221; (below 10, spelled out); &#8220;12-month,&#8221; &#8220;61%,&#8221; &#8220;38%,&#8221; &#8220;800,&#8221; &#8220;$1,200,&#8221; &#8220;$500,000&#8221; all use figures as required.</li> <li><strong>Attribution</strong>: &#8220;said Maria Reyes&#8221; — past tense, name before title, placed after the quote.</li> </ul> <p><a href="https://www.ereleases.com/press-release-sample/ap-style-for-press-releases-the-ultimate-guide/" data-wpel-link="internal">See more specific AP Style rules here</a>.</p> <h2 id="digital">AP Style for Digital Press Releases</h2> <p>While traditional AP style was developed for print, digital press releases require some adaptations:</p> <h3>Balancing SEO Needs with AP Style Requirements</h3> <p>Strategic keyword placement is essential for digital visibility:</p> <ul> <li>Include primary keywords in headline and first paragraph</li> <li>Use secondary keywords throughout but avoid keyword stuffing</li> <li>Maintain AP style even while incorporating keywords</li> <li>Consider using keywords in subheadings where appropriate</li> </ul> <h3>Hyperlinking Guidelines</h3> <p>AP style for hyperlinks:</p> <ul> <li>Hyperlink text should make sense out of context</li> <li>Don&#8217;t hyperlink entire paragraphs</li> <li>Avoid phrases like &#8220;click here&#8221;</li> <li>Don&#8217;t hyperlink the company name in the boilerplate section</li> </ul> <h3>Formatting for Email Distribution</h3> <p>When sending press releases via email:</p> <ul> <li>Include the release in the body of the email, not just as an attachment</li> <li>Use plain text or limited HTML formatting</li> <li>Create a compelling subject line that includes your company name</li> <li>Don&#8217;t use all caps or excessive punctuation in the subject line</li> </ul> <h3>AP Style for Multimedia Elements</h3> <p>When including multimedia assets:</p> <ul> <li>Provide captions for all images following AP style (brief, factual descriptions)</li> <li>Include photo credits where appropriate</li> <li>Don&#8217;t embed videos directly in email releases; instead, provide links</li> <li>Offer a downloadable <a href="https://www.ereleases.com/ultimate-press-kit-guide-small-businesses-definition-components-examples/" title="The Ultimate Press Kit Guide for Small Businesses: Definition, Components, and Examples for 2025" data-wpil-monitor-id="1728" data-wpel-link="internal">press kit</a> with high-resolution images</li> </ul> <h2 id="mobile">Mobile Optimization</h2> <p>With journalists and readers increasingly consuming content on mobile devices, ensuring your AP style press releases remain properly formatted for mobile viewing is essential:</p> <h3>Text Formatting for Mobile</h3> <ul> <li><strong>Shorter Paragraphs</strong>: Limit paragraphs to 2-3 sentences maximum for better readability on small screens</li> <li><strong>Bulleted Lists</strong>: Use bulleted lists for key points, which are more mobile-friendly than dense paragraphs</li> <li><strong>Subheadings</strong>: Incorporate brief subheadings to break up text and improve scannability</li> <li><strong>White Space</strong>: Increase white space between elements to improve mobile readability</li> </ul> <h3>Technical Considerations</h3> <ul> <li><strong>Responsive Design</strong>: When distributing via email, use responsive email templates that adapt to screen size</li> <li><strong>File Size</strong>: Keep any attached images under 1MB to ensure quick loading on mobile devices</li> <li><strong>Plain Text Alternative</strong>: Always include a plain text version alongside HTML for email distribution</li> <li><strong>Link Formatting</strong>: Make sure hyperlinks have sufficient space around them to prevent &#8220;fat finger&#8221; mis-clicks</li> </ul> <h3>Mobile-Friendly Multimedia</h3> <ul> <li><strong>Image Dimensions</strong>: Optimize images to display properly on mobile (800px width maximum)</li> <li><strong>Alt Text</strong>: Include AP style descriptions as alt text for all images</li> <li><strong>Video Links</strong>: Instead of embedding videos, provide links to mobile-friendly platforms</li> <li><strong>Infographics</strong>: Create mobile-specific versions of infographics with larger text and simpler layouts</li> </ul> <h3>Testing</h3> <ul> <li><strong>Device Testing</strong>: Preview your press release on multiple devices before distribution</li> <li><strong>Email Client Testing</strong>: Check formatting across major email clients used by journalists</li> <li><strong>Download Speed</strong>: Test download time for any attachments on typical mobile connections</li> </ul> <h2 id="seo">Incorporating SEO into AP Style Press Releases</h2> <p>One important strategic advantage of press releases is their tendency to improve your search engine ranking (SEO.) Balancing search engine optimization with AP style requirements requires strategic integration of keywords while maintaining journalistic standards:</p> <h3>Headline Optimization</h3> <p><strong>AP Style Only:</strong><br /> Company launches new software platform</p> <p><strong>AP Style with SEO:</strong><br /> Company launches new AI-powered inventory management software</p> <p>The SEO version incorporates specific keywords (AI-powered, inventory management) while maintaining AP style capitalization rules.</p> <h3>Lead Paragraph Optimization</h3> <p><strong>AP Style Only:</strong><br /> NEW YORK — A technology company released a new platform today aimed at helping businesses improve operations.</p> <p><strong>AP Style with SEO:</strong><br /> NEW YORK — XYZ Technology released its new cloud-based inventory management software today, designed to help small businesses reduce stockouts and overstock situations.</p> <p>The SEO version naturally incorporates target keywords (cloud-based inventory management, small businesses, stockouts) while maintaining the essential AP style elements.</p> <h3>Boilerplate Optimization</h3> <p><strong>AP Style Only:</strong><br /> About Company X:<br /> Company X provides software solutions for businesses. The company was founded in 2020 and is based in New York.</p> <p><strong>AP Style with SEO:</strong><br /> About XYZ Technology:<br /> XYZ Technology develops cloud-based inventory management software for small and medium-sized retailers. Founded in 2020 and headquartered in New York, the company helps more than 500 retailers optimize their inventory processes and reduce operational costs. Learn more at xyztechnology.com.</p> <p>The SEO version naturally incorporates target keywords and phrases throughout while maintaining AP style formatting.</p> <h3>Balancing SEO and AP Style Tips</h3> <ul> <li>Place primary keywords in the headline and first paragraph without forcing them</li> <li>Use secondary keywords in subsequent paragraphs where they fit naturally</li> <li>Maintain proper AP style for numbers, dates, and titles even when incorporating keywords</li> <li>Use industry-specific terms that journalists and searchers would both recognize</li> <li>Avoid keyword stuffing, which violates both SEO best practices and AP style&#8217;s emphasis on clear writing</li> </ul> <h2>The 10 Most Common AP Style Mistakes in Press Releases</h2> <p>Even careful writers make predictable errors when adapting AP style for press releases. Here are the ten mistakes journalists notice most — and how to fix them.</p> <h3>1. Writing out &#8220;percent&#8221; instead of using the % symbol. </h3> <p>AP style updated its guidance on this in 2019. Use the % symbol in all cases except at the start of a sentence. Correct: &#8220;Sales increased 14% year over year.&#8221; Incorrect: &#8220;Sales increased 14 percent year over year.&#8221;</p> <h3>2. Using ordinal indicators with dates.</h3> <p>AP style never uses 1st, 2nd, 3rd, or 4th with dates. Write &#8220;The event takes place Jan. 15&#8221; — not &#8220;January 15th.&#8221; The month abbreviation rules also catch many writers off guard: only Jan., Feb., Aug., Sept., Oct., Nov., and Dec. are abbreviated. March, April, May, June, and July are always spelled out.</p> <h3>3. Capitalizing job titles that follow a name.</h3> <p>Capitalize titles only when they directly precede a full name: &#8220;Chief Executive Officer Jane Smith announced&#8230;&#8221; But lowercase when the title follows the name or stands alone: &#8220;Jane Smith, chief executive officer, announced&#8230;&#8221; or &#8220;The chief executive officer said&#8230;&#8221; This rule trips up nearly every first-time press release writer.</p> <h3>4. Using &#8220;says&#8221; instead of &#8220;said&#8221; in quote attribution.</h3> <p>AP style uses past tense for attribution. Write &#8220;said Jane Smith&#8221; — not &#8220;says Jane Smith,&#8221; &#8220;stated,&#8221; &#8220;noted,&#8221; &#8220;explained,&#8221; or &#8220;exclaimed.&#8221; The word &#8220;said&#8221; is invisible to readers in a way that fancier attribution verbs are not, which is exactly why AP style insists on it.</p> <h3>5. Spelling out numbers 10 and above.</h3> <p>Spell out one through nine; use figures for 10 and above. However, always use figures — regardless of size — for ages, percentages, dimensions, temperatures, speeds, monetary amounts, and addresses. A sentence like &#8220;The five-year-old company employs 12 people across three offices&#8221; is fully correct AP style.</p> <h3>6. Using the Oxford comma. </h3> <p>AP style omits the serial comma (the comma before &#8220;and&#8221; or &#8220;or&#8221; in a list) unless it is needed to prevent confusion. Write &#8220;The company offers services in marketing, social media and web design&#8221; — not &#8220;marketing, social media, and web design.&#8221; The exception: use the Oxford comma when omitting it creates genuine ambiguity.</p> <h3>7. Abbreviating state names incorrectly. </h3> <p>AP uses its own state abbreviations — not the two-letter USPS postal codes. California is Calif., not CA. Texas is Texas (never abbreviated). Florida is Fla., not FL. These are distinct from postal codes, and journalists will notice the difference immediately.</p> <h3>8. Starting the lead with the company name.</h3> <p>A dateline-style press release should open with the news, not the company. &#8220;CHICAGO — Acme Corp. today announced&#8230;&#8221; buries the news behind the brand. Stronger: &#8220;CHICAGO — A new cloud accounting tool designed for sole proprietors launches today&#8230;&#8221; Lead with what happened; attribute it in the second or third sentence.</p> <h3>9. Writing the boilerplate in first person.</h3> <p>The &#8220;About [Company]&#8221; section should always be written in the third person. Avoid &#8220;We are a digital marketing agency.&#8221; Write instead: &#8220;Acme is a digital marketing agency founded in 2012.&#8221; Journalists may lift boilerplate language directly, and first-person language sounds odd in a news context.</p> <h3>10. Forgetting the end mark. </h3> <p>Every press release ends with ### centered on its own line. This is not decorative — it is a signal to journalists (and wire services) that the release is complete and that nothing was cut off in transmission. Omitting it looks amateurish to anyone who has read a press release before.</p> <h2>Press Release Best Practices for 2026</h2> <p>AP style rules are the foundation of an effective press release, but following them alone does not guarantee coverage. These best practices reflect both journalistic standards and how press releases are distributed and discovered in 2026.</p> <h3>Before You Write</h3> <ul> <li>Determine whether your news is genuinely newsworthy. Ask: Would a journalist covering your industry care about this if they heard about it from someone other than your company? If the honest answer is no, consider whether a blog post or social update is a better fit than a press release.</li> <li>Identify your target audience before writing. A release for a trade publication requires different framing than one aimed at the general business press.</li> <li>Choose one angle and build the release around it. Trying to announce multiple things in one release dilutes both the news value and the SEO focus.</li> </ul> <h3>Writing the Release</h3> <ul> <li>Lead with the most important information. Journalists scan releases in seconds — if the news is not in the first sentence, many will not find it.</li> <li>Write the headline last. Once the body is complete, you will have a clearer sense of the single strongest angle.</li> <li>Use one quote per release, with a maximum of two. Quotes should add perspective that the body copy cannot — not restate facts that are already there.</li> <li>Keep the release to 400-600 words. Longer releases are read less frequently and are harder to repurpose for news coverage.</li> <li>Use active voice. Passive constructions slow down the read and are more likely to be rewritten or cut by a journalist on deadline.</li> </ul> <h3>Distribution and Timing</h3> <ul> <li>Send press releases on Tuesday, Wednesday, or Thursday, between 10 a.m. and 2 p.m. in the target journalist&#8217;s time zone. Mondays and Fridays see lower pickup rates, and releases sent after 3 p.m. are more likely to be missed before the end of the day.</li> <li>Embargo releases strategically. If you need to give journalists lead time, an embargo can be appropriate, but set a specific embargo lift time and honor it exactly.</li> <li>Follow up once, not repeatedly. A single follow-up email 48 to 72 hours after distribution is professional; multiple follow-ups damage the relationship.</li> </ul> <h3>SEO and Digital Visibility</h3> <ul> <li>Place your primary keyword naturally in the headline and the first paragraph. Do not force it. If it reads awkwardly, rewrite the sentence.</li> <li>Include at least one internal link to a related resource on your website. This supports both SEO and the journalist&#8217;s ability to learn more about your company.</li> <li>Add alt text to any images distributed with the release. Image search drives meaningful referral traffic, and AP style image captions make good alt text with minor adaptation.</li> <li>Update evergreen press release content — like this page — when guidance changes. Stale examples and outdated rules reduce credibility with journalists and search engines alike.</li> </ul> <h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="h-general-questions">General Questions</h2> <div class="schema-faq wp-block-yoast-faq-block"><div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749226908590"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">How strict should I be about following AP style in my press releases?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">For press releases intended for media distribution, you should follow AP style as closely as possible. Journalists are accustomed to this style and may view inconsistencies as unprofessional. That said, it’s acceptable to have a few company-specific style preferences (such as how your company name is styled) as long as they’re applied consistently.</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749227021780"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">What’s the most important AP style rule to follow in press releases?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">The inverted pyramid structure — placing the most important information in the first paragraph — is the most critical AP style principle for press releases.<br /><br />Beyond structure, the rules journalists notice most immediately are proper formatting of dates (abbreviated months, no ordinal suffixes), numbers (spell out one through nine, figures for 10 and above), and quote attribution (past tense &#8220;said,&#8221; full name and title on first reference). Getting these right signals professional credibility before a journalist reads beyond the lead.</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749227051926"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">Do journalists actually care if my press release follows AP style?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">Yes. Journalists receive dozens or hundreds of press releases daily. Those that follow AP style require less editing and signal professionalism. Many journalists will immediately notice style errors, which can detract from your message or even lead them to dismiss your release entirely.</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749227114414"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">If I don’t have an AP Stylebook, where can I find reliable AP style information?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">While purchasing the AP Stylebook is the best option, the Purdue Online Writing Lab (OWL) offers a free summary of AP style basics. Many journalism schools and media organizations also publish&nbsp;<a href="https://www.ereleases.com/ap-style-what-why-it-matters/" data-wpel-link="internal">free AP style cheat sheets online</a>. Just ensure you’re looking at current information, as AP style rules are updated annually.</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749227151232"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">Should I follow AP style for press releases distributed internationally?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">Yes — AP style&#8217;s core principles of clarity, brevity, and consistent formatting are valuable for international audiences, but add context where needed.<br /><br />For international releases, include metric equivalents in parentheses after U.S. measurements, clarify regional terms that may not translate, and spell out acronyms that are industry-standard in the U.S. but unfamiliar abroad. Monetary figures should specify the currency clearly on first reference. The dateline city still follows AP style, even for releases originating outside the United States.</p> </div> </div> <h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="h-formatting-and-structure">Formatting and Structure</h2> <div class="schema-faq wp-block-yoast-faq-block"><div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749227282071"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">How long should an AP style press release be?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">An AP style press release should be 400 to 600 words — approximately one page.<br /><br />This length follows the journalistic principle of brevity while providing enough detail for a complete story. If additional information is genuinely necessary, include it in a &#8220;Notes to Editors&#8221; section below the boilerplate, or link to a supporting press kit. Anything beyond 600 words is rarely read in full and is unlikely to be reprinted as written.</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749227314739"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">Is there a specific font or formatting required for printed AP style press releases?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">AP style doesn’t specify exact font requirements, but the industry standard is to use Times New Roman or Arial at 12-point size with single spacing and left alignment. Avoid decorative fonts, colored text, or excessive formatting that might distract from your message.</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749227344885"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">Should I include images with my AP style press release?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">While AP style doesn’t address image inclusion specifically, it’s beneficial to include relevant high-quality images with your press release. Provide these as separate attachments (not embedded) with AP style captions that identify people from left to right and include relevant context.</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749227377439"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">How should I format quotes in an AP style press release?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">Attribute quotes to specific individuals with their full name and title on first reference (just last name on subsequent references). Use “said” rather than “says,” “stated,” or other alternatives. Place the attribution after the quote, and remember that periods and commas go inside quotation marks.</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749227405537"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">How should I structure the headline in AP style?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">AP style headlines capitalize only the first word and proper nouns. Keep headlines concise (ideally under 80-100 characters) and focused on the key news element. Avoid clickbait, questions, or excessive punctuation.</p> </div> </div> <h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="h-common-ap-style-questions-for-press-releases">Common AP Style Questions for Press Releases</h2> <div class="schema-faq wp-block-yoast-faq-block"><div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749227483169"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">Should I use the Oxford comma in my press release?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">AP style generally avoids the Oxford comma (the comma before “and” or “or” in a series), but allows it when necessary for clarity. For example: “The company offers services in marketing, social media and web design” (no Oxford comma) vs. “The CEO thanked her parents, Oprah Winfrey, and Michelle Obama” (Oxford comma used to clarify that her parents are not Oprah and Michelle).</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749227522206"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">How do I format dates in AP style?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">Abbreviate months that are six or more letters long when they appear with a specific date: Jan., Feb., Aug., Sept., Oct., Nov., Dec.<br /><br />March, April, May, June, and July are never abbreviated. Do not use ordinal indicators (1st, 2nd, 3rd) with dates. When the year appears mid-sentence, set it off with commas: &#8220;The product launched Jan. 15, 2025, and has since&#8230;&#8221; When writing a month and year without a specific date, spell out the full month: &#8220;The report, published in September 2024, found&#8230;&#8221;</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749227552920"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">How should I handle numbers in my press release?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">Spell out numbers one through nine; use figures for 10 and above.<br /><br />Always use figures — regardless of the number — for ages, percentages (using the % symbol), dimensions, temperatures, speeds, monetary amounts, addresses, and any statistical data. A correct AP style sentence might read: &#8220;The five-year-old company employs 12 people across three cities and reported $2.4 million in revenue last year.&#8221; Note that &#8220;5-year-old&#8221; (an age) uses a figure, while &#8220;three cities&#8221; (a count below 10) is spelled out.</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749227613547"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">How do I properly format my company’s name if it doesn’t follow AP style rules?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">AP style generally respects a company’s preferred styling of its name, even when it breaks standard rules. If your company name includes unusual capitalization (SalesForce), no spaces (LinkedIn), or special punctuation (Yahoo!), maintain that styling throughout your press release.</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749227640399"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">Should I spell out acronyms on first reference?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">In general, spell out acronyms on first reference followed by the acronym in parentheses, then use just the acronym for subsequent references. Widely recognized acronyms like FBI, NASA, or CEO don’t need to be spelled out. Industry-specific acronyms should always be spelled out on first reference.</p> </div> </div> <h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="h-digital-considerations">Digital Considerations</h2> <div class="schema-faq wp-block-yoast-faq-block"><div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749227869952"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">How do I adapt AP style for digital press releases?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">For digital press releases, all core AP style rules still apply. Additional considerations include using SEO-friendly headlines while maintaining AP style capitalization, creating shorter paragraphs for screen readability, and properly formatting hyperlinks (use descriptive anchor text, not “click here”).</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749227906233"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">Should hyperlinks be styled differently in an AP style press release?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">AP style doesn’t provide specific guidance on hyperlink formatting, but best practices include:<br />&#8211; Don’t hyperlink entire paragraphs or sentences<br />&#8211; Use descriptive anchor text rather than raw URLs or phrases like “click here”<br />&#8211; When sharing raw URLs in a press release, include the http:// or https:// prefix</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749227962657"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">How should I handle multimedia elements in digital press releases?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">For digital press releases, describe available multimedia elements at the end of the release rather than embedding them. For example: “High-resolution images, B-roll footage, and executive headshots are available in this digital press kit: [link].” Provide captions for all visual elements following AP style guidelines.</p> </div> </div> <h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="h-industry-specific-questions">Industry-Specific Questions</h2> <div class="schema-faq wp-block-yoast-faq-block"><div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749228048887"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">How do I format product names that don’t follow standard capitalization?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">Follow the company’s official styling of product names, even when they break AP style rules (iPhone, WordPerfect). If the product name begins a sentence, either rewrite the sentence or capitalize normally: “IPhone users will notice improvements” would be incorrect; instead write “iPhone users will notice improvements” or “Users of iPhone will notice improvements.”</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749228081370"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">Are there specific AP style guidelines for technical press releases?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">Follow the company&#8217;s official styling of product names, even when they violate standard capitalization rules.<br /><br />AP style respects trade names, so iPhone, WordPress, YouTube, and similar names retain their unconventional capitalization throughout the release. The one practical complication is sentence-starting: if a product name beginning with a lowercase letter would open a sentence, rewrite the sentence to avoid starting with it. Write &#8220;Users of iPhone noticed&#8230;&#8221; rather than &#8220;IPhone users noticed&#8230;&#8221; — the latter looks like a typo.</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749228108488"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">How should financial figures be handled in AP style press releases?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">Use figures for all monetary amounts. Use the $ sign for dollars, spell out “cents” for amounts under a dollar, and use decimals for even dollar amounts only when they appear with amounts that include cents. For example: “$5,” “50 cents,” “$5.25,” and “$5 million” are all correct AP style.</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749228135779"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">Is AP style different for crisis communication press releases?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">The core AP style rules remain the same for crisis communications, though these releases typically emphasize straightforward, factual information with less marketing language. Attribution becomes particularly important; clearly identify who is making statements about the crisis situation.</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749228166519"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">How should I handle job titles in an AP style press release?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">Capitalize job titles when they precede a name, but lowercase when they follow a name or stand alone. For example: “Chief Executive Officer Jane Smith announced…” but “Jane Smith, chief executive officer, announced…” or “The chief executive officer announced…”</p> </div> </div> <h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="h-working-with-ereleases">Working with eReleases</h2> <div class="schema-faq wp-block-yoast-faq-block"><div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749228238810"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">How does eReleases ensure my press release follows AP style?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">eReleases has editors who review all press releases for AP style compliance before distribution. They can catch common errors and suggest corrections to ensure your release meets professional standards, saving you from potential embarrassment and improving your chances of media pickup.</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749228270582"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">Will eReleases edit my press release if it doesn’t follow AP style?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">eReleases offers professional editing services that include AP style corrections. For minor issues, their editors may make adjustments automatically. For more significant revisions, they’ll work with you to bring your release into compliance with AP style guidelines while preserving your key messages.</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749228301130"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">Can eReleases help create an AP style press release from scratch?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">Yes,&nbsp;<a href="https://www.ereleases.com/press-release-writing/" data-wpel-link="internal">eReleases offers press release writing services</a>&nbsp;from experienced writers who are well-versed in AP style. Their team can craft professional press releases that adhere to all relevant guidelines while effectively communicating your news to target media.</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749228324552"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">How important is AP style for press release distribution success?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">Very important. eReleases distributes to journalists who expect professionally formatted content. Press releases that follow AP style demonstrate media savvy and respect for journalists’ time, significantly increasing the likelihood of coverage. eReleases’ data shows that properly formatted releases typically receive more media attention than those with style inconsistencies.</p> </div> <div class="schema-faq-section" id="faq-question-1749228349845"><h3 class="schema-faq-question">Does eReleases offer resources to help me learn AP style?</h3> <p class="schema-faq-answer">eReleases provides clients with style guides and tips for creating effective press releases. Their blog also features articles about AP style best practices. Additionally, their editing team can provide feedback that helps you improve your understanding of AP style for future releases.</p> </div> </div> <h2>Professional Press Release Distribution with eReleases</h2> <p>Creating an AP-style press release is only half the battle—the other half is getting it into the right hands.</p> <h3>Why Professional Distribution Matters</h3> <p>Even the most perfectly formatted press release won&#8217;t generate coverage if it doesn&#8217;t reach the right journalists. eReleases solves this problem by:</p> <ul> <li>Targeting industry-specific journalists who cover your niche</li> <li>Providing access to premium distribution networks typically only available to large corporations</li> <li>Offering placement on major news sites and industry publications</li> <li>Including guaranteed distribution through PR Newswire, reaching thousands of newsrooms</li> </ul> <h3>How eReleases Maximizes Your AP-Style Press Release</h3> <p>eReleases ensures your carefully crafted press release achieves maximum impact:</p> <ul> <li>Expert editors review your release to confirm AP style compliance</li> <li>Strategic distribution to relevant journalists based on your industry and target market</li> <li>Detailed reports showing who received and engaged with your release</li> <li>Syndication across online news platforms to boost SEO and visibility</li> </ul> <h3>Special Offer for Small Businesses</h3> <p>Get your AP-style press release in front of the right journalists with <a href="https://www.ereleases.com/pricing/" data-wpel-link="internal">eReleases&#8217; small business package</a>:</p> <ul> <li>Professional distribution to targeted media lists</li> <li>AP style review and editing assistance</li> <li>Guaranteed placement on major news sites</li> <li>Comprehensive reporting on your release&#8217;s performance</li> </ul> <h3>Take the Next Step</h3> <p>Visit eReleases.com today to ensure your AP-style press release gets maximum visibility with the journalists who matter to your business. With eReleases, your small business can achieve the media coverage it deserves.</p><div class="sabox-authors"><div class="saboxplugin-wrap" itemtype="http://schema.org/Person" itemscope itemprop="author"><div class="saboxplugin-tab saboxplugin-tab-about"><div class="saboxplugin-gravatar"><a href="https://www.ereleases.com/about-mickie-kennedy/" data-wpel-link="internal"><img width="200" height="200" alt="Mickie Kennedy, Founder and CEO of eReleases" itemprop="image" nitro-lazy-src="https://cdn-eaebb.nitrocdn.com/QvzDcOKmPZNTGhotvWgVpUsyPfCTqwim/assets/images/optimized/rev-e978aec/media.ereleases.com/uploads/2026/04/mek.png" content="https://cdn-eaebb.nitrocdn.com/QvzDcOKmPZNTGhotvWgVpUsyPfCTqwim/assets/images/optimized/rev-e978aec/media.ereleases.com/uploads/2026/04/mek.png" class="nitro-lazy" decoding="async" nitro-lazy-empty id="MjIyMDo2NzE=-1" data-nitro-empty-id="MjIyMDo2NzE=-1" src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB2aWV3Qm94PSIwIDAgNDc1IDYyMiIgd2lkdGg9IjQ3NSIgaGVpZ2h0PSI2MjIiIHhtbG5zPSJodHRwOi8vd3d3LnczLm9yZy8yMDAwL3N2ZyI+PC9zdmc+" /></a></div><div class="saboxplugin-authorname"><a href="https://www.ereleases.com/about-mickie-kennedy/" class="vcard author" rel="author" itemprop="url" data-wpel-link="internal"><span class="fn" itemprop="name">Mickie Kennedy</span></a></div><div class="saboxplugin-desc"><div itemprop="description"><p>Mickie Kennedy founded <a href="https://www.ereleases.com/" data-wpel-link="internal">eReleases.com</a> in 1998 after watching PR firms charge small businesses a $20,000 minimum for a single press release. For 27 years, he&#8217;s been closing that gap — distributing 150,000+ releases via Cision PR Newswire, serving 30,000+ clients including Manscaped, Squatty Potty, and 40+ Shark Tank brands, and generating $132M+ in earned media. A sought-after podcast guest and two-time Amazon bestselling author of <a href="https://www.amazon.com/dp/B00OI0L1Q0" data-wpel-link="external" rel="external noopener"><i>Beginner&#8217;s Guide to Writing Powerful Press Releases</i></a> and<a href="https://www.amazon.com/dp/B091S9SRPR" data-wpel-link="external" rel="external noopener"> <i>The PR Strategy Manifesto</i></a>. Mickie holds an MFA in Creative Writing from George Mason University. Based in Baltimore, MD. <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/publicity" data-wpel-link="external" rel="external noopener"> LinkedIn</a></p> </div></div><div class="clearfix"></div></div></div></div>
